市場焦點
美國
公債回購
• 美國財政部9月9日宣布，將於翌日回購最高60億美元的10至20年期美債，低於部分市場期待
的80億至100億美元規模 。民間持有美債規模達 31.8兆美元，相較之下 ，本次60億美元回購
僅約占民間持債規模 0.02%，對整體影響有限 。此外，約6成的美債期限不足 5年，意味未來
再融資需求仍高，若財政赤字未明顯收斂，長端殖利率上行壓力仍存。
日本央行
• 日本第二季GDP年化季增率上修至 1.4%(優於初值1.1%)，儘管伊朗戰爭引發原油供應緊張給
經濟帶來壓力，但GDP增速依舊高於0.7%潛在經濟成長率，雖然經濟表現支持日本央行(BOJ)
逐步實施貨幣政策正常化 ，但更關鍵的催化劑來自日美兩國協調干預匯市 ，遏制日圓極端貶
值的政治壓力，彭博經濟研究預期日本央行將在9月會議上加息25個基點。

美國
通膨
• 美國8月生產者物價指數 （PPI）年增5.4%，略高市場預期的 5.3%，核心PPI年增4.6%，符合
預期。8月消費者物價指數（CPI）年增3.35%、核心CPI年增2.4%，均符合市場預期，但核心
CPI月升幅0.3%，高於市場預期的 0.2%。數據公佈後，FedWatch顯示市場預期9月升息機率，
從前一周的50%攀升至86%。

美國8月通膨數據(CPI)
• 美國8月通膨數據：8月美國CPI月增0.4%、年增3.4%，核心CPI月增0.3%，核心服務通膨升溫至0.5%，顯示通膨降溫仍不
均衡，能源價格上漲為主要推手，市場預估8月核心PCE月增約0.3%、年增約3.8%，仍高於Fed 2%目標，搭配強勁就業數
據，9月升息1碼的壓力升高。不過，本次通膨主要來自能源、需求及AI投資帶動，與2021～2022年供給型通膨有所不同。
• 市場反應與展望：CPI公布後，2年期美債殖利率一度上升，惟長天期殖利率反而走低，顯示市場關注焦點並非單純升息，而
是Fed能否維持抗通膨政策可信度。整體而言，升息未必等同Risk-off，若經濟與企業獲利維持韌性，股市仍具支撐。
資料來源： investing.com，2026.9.14

能源成本回升推升PPI，通膨壓力牽動Fed政策空間
• 能源成本回升，PPI重新升溫：美國8月PPI月增0.4%、年增5.4%，增幅均明顯高於7月，主要受到商品價格上漲帶動；其
中最終需求商品價格月增1.1%，能源價格上升4.2%，柴油更是大漲逾2成，成為商品價格上升的重要來源，惟最終需求服
務價格僅略增0.1%，顯示本次PPI升溫仍以能源及商品價格為主。
• 成本壓力仍在，利率政策不確定性升高： 扣除食品、能源及貿易服務後的核心 PPI月增0.3%，微低於7月的0.4%，但年增
4.7%，顯示價格壓力仍未完全消退；其中運輸與倉儲服務價格上升2.3%，反映部分成本仍具黏著性，致使市場對Fed利率
政策的重新定價明顯升溫，進而推動股、債市短線波動擴大。
• 市場觀察：由於本次PPI升溫主要來自能源成本 ，但服務價格壓力整體仍相對溫和 ，因此尚不足以視為通膨全面升溫 ，然

能源價格若持續走高 ，將提高Fed維持較高利率的壓力 ，建議投資人短線宜降低長天期債券與高估值成長股的追價 ，債券
可優先配置短中天期、高品質債券，股票則可關注能源、金融及具定價能力企業，提高投組對利率與成本波動的承受度。
資料來源：U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics，Producer Price Indexes – Aug,  2026，2026.9.11
季調後PPI最終需求價格指數月變動
資料來源：Bloomberg， 2026.9.11

• 財政部對美債的回購規模不如預期：美國財政部9月9日宣布，將於翌日回購最高60億美元的10至20年期美債，主要鎖定流

動性較低的舊券，以改善長天期債券市場流動性。雖然本次回購高於先前設定的每次至少40億美元，但低於部分市場期待的
80億至100億美元規模。消息公布後10年期公債殖利率逼近5%，30年期殖利率亦突破5.3%。
• 回購規模在高額赤字背景下，難以有效拉低長天期公債殖利率：美國今年公債發行規模較2025年增加11.8%，民間持有美債
規模達31.8兆美元、年增8.2%，相較之下，本次60億美元回購僅約占民間持債規模0.02%，對整體影響有限。此外，約6成
的美債期限不足5年，意味未來再融資需求仍高，若財政赤字未明顯收斂，長端殖利率上行壓力仍存。反觀新興國家基本面
持續改善，且當地債市有貨幣潛在升值空間，可作為全球債券配置的分散來源。

地緣政治動盪推升油價，但全球經濟依然展現韌性
市場共識未來12 個月預期每股盈餘

全球主要市場及國家經濟成長率
新興市場
美股
日股
歐股
• 地緣政治動盪推升油價，但全球經濟依然展現韌性：美國和伊朗的衝突已經進入第7個月，能源價格上升及地緣政治風險仍
對全球經濟與市場信心形成壓力，但AI投資熱潮持續推升科技需求，使全球經濟展現優於預期的韌性。其中，亞洲仍是全球
成長動能最強的區域之一，2026年亞洲（不含日本）經濟成長率預估達4.7%，明顯高於全球平均2.9%及成熟市場1.6%。在
內需與科技出口支撐下，新興亞洲基本面相對穩健，具備較佳的中長期配置條件。

• AI資本支出延續，台韓成為核心受惠市場：AI資本支出持續擴張，2026年全球雲端AI資本支出預估達7,960億美元，並帶動
AI半導體市場至2030年突破7,500億美元。亞洲掌握全球半導體供應鏈核心，台灣受惠於先進製程與先進封裝需求，韓國則
受惠於HBM記憶體需求，台韓可望成為AI基礎建設擴張的主要受惠市場；同時，中國與印度則提供較穩定的內需及經濟成
長支撐。2026年亞洲企業盈餘成長率預估達72.13%，顯著高於全球平均，建議新興亞洲以台韓科技股為主要成長配置，並
搭配中國、印度市場分散區域風險。

顧
短債供給增加未引發融資緊張，短天期債券防禦價值提升
• 回購市場維持穩定，短債供給暫未造成壓力：美國回購市場今年以來維持平穩，儘管9月短債償付與企業繳稅可能帶來短
暫資金波動，但SOFR與IORB利差仍處相對正常區間 ，顯示目前準備金與短期流動性尚稱充裕 。此外SOFR/IORB差值對
短期美債淨發行增加的敏感度明顯低於2025年底，反映流動性環境偏寬鬆。
• 貨幣基金成長放緩，供給吸收能力受到限制：零售資金流入偏弱，使貨幣市場基金資產增速落後 ，2026年迄今僅增加約
325億美元，遠低於2025年同期的2,050億美元；即便Q4季節性回升，新增資金對短債供給的承接能力仍可能有限。
• 後市觀察：年底前短債淨發行規模預估約3,250億美元，後續需關注短債供給、企業繳稅及財政部長端公債回購對資金面

的影響。建議投資人配置上可提高 1～3年期美國國庫券或短天期美債 ETF比重，以取得票息收益並降低長端殖利率波動
對債券價格的影響。
資料來源：US Treasury，FRBNY ，Federal Reserve ，2026.9.9
美債淨供應 vs. SOFR/IORB差值
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.9

風險偏好升溫與債券供給增加，推升期限溢價與長債殖利率
• 全球期限溢酬上升，長債面臨重新定價：全球債券殖利率同步走高，不僅反映各國政策利率預期，也受到全球期限溢酬上升
推動；投資人對持有長天期政府債券要求更高補償 ，顯示長債殖利率上行已具有跨市場的結構性因素 。Bloomberg
Economics的模型亦顯示，全球期限溢酬是近年G7長債殖利率走高的重要共同因素。
• 風險資產吸引資金，放大長債供需壓力：AI投資題材推升股票市場、信用利差仍相對偏窄，加上大宗商品走強，使股票、公
司債及商品與政府債券形成資金競爭；在政府融資需求持續增加的情況下 ，若債券需求未同步擴張，政府公債需提供更高殖
利率才能吸引資金。
• 後市觀察：長端利率已成為股債配置的重要變數 ，若殖利率因財政供給與風險偏好維持高檔 ，長天期債券及高估值股票均可

能承壓；建議以短中天期美債及高品質投資級公司債為核心 ，待長端殖利率充分反映期限溢酬後，再分批拉長債券存續期間。
資料來源：Bloomberg Economics，2026.9.9

日本央行升息箭在弦上，但缺乏連續升息條件
• GDP修正支持升息 ，但關鍵點在配合日本和美國政府遏制日圓疲軟： 日本第二季 GDP年化季增率上修至 1.4%(優於初值
1.1%)，儘管伊朗戰爭引發原油供應緊張給經濟帶來壓力，但GDP增速依舊高於0.7%潛在經濟成長率，雖然經濟表現支持日
本央行(BOJ)逐步實施貨幣政策正常化 ，但更關鍵的催化劑來自日美兩國協調干預匯市 ，遏制日圓極端貶值的政治壓力 ，彭
博經濟研究預期日本央行將在9月會議上加息25個基點。
• 缺乏連續大幅加息的支撐力道： (1)經濟衝擊風險：日圓升值效應已對經濟產生緊縮效果 ，彭博預估可能拖累實際 GDP成長
達0.3%。(2)財政負擔過重：若政策利率升至 1.75%以上，將大幅增加政府利息支出 ，擠壓高市內閣的財政刺激空間 。(3)企

業投資放緩：私人資本支出仍顯疲軟(季比下降0.9%)，顯示經濟復甦基礎尚未完全穩固。
• 小結：日本央行在政治、匯率與經濟基本面交集下 ，9月升息已成定局，但考量財政負擔與全球金融穩定後 ，其後續加息步
伐應偏向謹慎與循序漸進 ，密切關注9月日本央行會議後聲明 ，若央行強調循序漸進而非連續緊縮 ，市場波動相對可控；反
之，若釋放過度鷹派訊號，則需防範市場拋售風險。由於市場資金對日圓套利交易依賴度仍高，日圓任何短期內的急速升值，
都極易觸發全球風險資產的連鎖去槓桿效應。

中國8月通膨回升，惟需求端疲弱
• 8月份 CPI及PPI：消費者物 價指 數 (CPI)月增 0.4%(前月月減 0.1%)、年增 0.8%(前月 0.5%)，生 產者物價指數 (PPI) 月增
0.4%(前月月減0.7%)、年增3.8%(前月3.5%)。通膨走揚主要受到能源及通訊設備成本上升影響。
• 觀點：中國自2023年以來，內需市場面臨房地產崩盤 、青年失業率飆升以及公共債務攀升等困境 。今年年初以來，通膨雖
然由負轉正，不過從細項得知，因國際油價飆升、AI硬體設備面臨漲價壓力，本波通膨回升主要是受到供給端影響。外部成
本壓力恐進一步壓抑中國內需消費，中國要在真正意義上走出通縮環境可能還需要更多官方政策發力。彭博預估，在需求未
出現明顯改善的情境下，中國央行需要近一步放寬貨幣政策，預測年底前可能降息10個基點。

資料來源：Bloomberg，截至2026.9.9。

AI產業獲利亮眼，但需留意變現能力和資本效率
• AI獲利增速領先大盤，銷售成長成為獲利關鍵：AI板塊在2026年第一與第二季分別實現52.8%與59.7%的高獲利成長，受高
基期影響，未來四季度平均獲利成長預計放緩至 34.4%，但仍大幅優於非 AI板塊12.3%。預計2026年第三季至2027年第二
季，AI平均銷售成長率將達 27.2%，為非AI成分股(5%)5倍以上 。隨著未來利潤率擴張放緩 (預計未來 6個季度平均僅提升
1.66%，低於過去12個季度的3.52%)，持續的銷售成長是維持獲利的關鍵。
• AI供應鏈內部發展並不均衡： 半導體最為強勁，2026Q3至2027Q2預期銷售成長65.8%，獲利大增112.1%且淨利潤率大幅
擴張8.86%，為產業鏈核心引擎。雲端巨頭表現穩健，營收與獲利增速維持約26%，基礎設施軟體與硬體也有15%~20%營

收與獲利成長 。但應用端軟體成長相對乏力 ，未來四季度獲利預期增速僅 4.3%與6.5%，表現低於標普500非AI股平均水準。
• 資本強度創新高 ，但自由現金流承壓： AI資本支出佔營收比重自 2024年Q1的8.9%一路攀升至 2026年Q2的20.1%，預計
2026年下半年將達到23%的高點。資本支出激增導致自由現金流與資本支出之比(FCF/Capex)從2.48倍滑落至0.69倍，自由
現金流利潤率亦從21%降至13.9%。隨著現金逐漸消耗，企業可能需透過債券市場進行發債融資 ，以維持龐大基礎設施投資。
• 小結與投資建議：AI板塊依然是美股獲利驅動力，但隨利潤率提升空間受限、自由現金流受資本支出壓制，投資邏輯已從全
面暴漲轉向注重變現能力與資本效率。投資建議聚焦上游硬體與半導體，建議持續優選具備高定價能力與實質訂單支撐的半
導體與雲端基礎設施龍頭，留意資本支出佔比過高且自由現金流急劇惡化公司，優先選擇現金流充沛、融資成本低企業。
標普500指數盈利成長：AI vs. 非AI
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.7

標普500指數剔除AI後利潤率或下降

銀行業擴大收益來源， 建議投資人可以適度配置，增加投組多元性。

Fed能否維持抗通膨政策可信度。整體而言，升息未必等同Risk-off，若經濟與企業獲利維持韌性，股市仍具支撐。

付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良

息日前一日之淨值×100%。

### Page 1

Blue Owl Capital 关联⽅为 CoreWeave 数据中⼼发⾏的 11 亿美元⾼收益债券，以 9.25% 收
益率完成定价 —— 较同评级均值⾼出 270 个基点。 76 兆⽡算⼒全部押注 CoreWeave ⼀家、
15 年合同锁定 29.4 亿美元现⾦流，标普直指 " 单⼀投机级租⼾敞⼝ " 为关键⻛险。这笔交
易，正为 AI 基础设施融资成本重新划定底线。
摘要：
Blue Owl Capital 关联⽅为  CoreWeave 相关数据中⼼项⽬发⾏的  11 亿美元⾼收益债券，以
9.25% 的收益率完成定价，较同类评级债券平均收益率⾼出约  2.7 个百分点。这笔交易为  AI 算
⼒热潮的信⽤⻛险标出了新价格：当数据中⼼的租⼾本⾝是投机级借款⼈时，融资成本正在被
市场重新定价。
据知情⼈⼠，这批  5 年期债券定价于⾯值的  98.5% ，由⾼盛、花旗和德意志银⾏联合承销；募
集资⾦将⽤于弗吉尼亚州⾥⼠满附近  Digital Drive 数据中⼼园区的额外算⼒建设。该设施  76 兆

⽡  IT 容量已全部预租给  CoreWeave ，合同期  15 年、总值  29.4 亿美元，预计  2027 ⾄  2028 年
间投运。
标普全球评级周⼆将该笔发⾏评为  BB- ，较投资级低三档，并明确指出 " 对单⼀投机级租⼾的敞
⼝仍是关键⻛险 " 。这笔交易恰好发⽣在  AI 基础设施债务⼤量涌⼊⾼收益债市场、投资者对缺乏
投资级租⼾的项⽬要求更⾼收益率的时点， CoreWeave 正是典型。
CoreWeave 作为  AI 算⼒新贵，⼆季度营收同⽐增⻓逾⼀倍⾄  25.75 亿美元、积压订单达  1040
亿美元，但⾼增⻓并未消除债券投资者对其⻓期偿债能⼒的疑虑。这笔定价或将成为后续  AI 数
据中⼼项⽬融资成本的参照系。
⾼额溢价背后：市场为单⼀租⼾⻛险开价
据知情⼈⼠向彭博透露，此次发⾏的五年期票据定价为 98.5 美分，收益率 9.25% ，较彭博追踪的
同评级债券平均收益率⾼出约 270 个基点。
这⼀溢价幅度直接反映了项⽬的结构性⻛险：底层资产的全部 76 兆⽡ IT 容量将完全租赁给

CoreWeave ⼀家，租期 15 年，合同总⾦额 29.4 亿美元，预计于 2027 年⾄ 2028 年间投⼊运营。单
⼀租⼾、单⼀项⽬、单⼀来源现⾦流，三重集中令投资者要求显著⾼于市场均值的⻛险补偿。
标普在报告中将该债务评为 BB- ，并将 " 对单⼀投机级租⼾的敞⼝ " 列为关键⻛险项。这意味着，
哪怕 CoreWeave 的租约条款在账⾯上看似稳健，评级机构仍对其信⽤资质所带来的潜在波动保
持警惕。
CoreWeave 的信⽤成⾊： AI 算⼒新贵的两⾯性
CoreWeave 是近年崛起最快的 AI 算⼒基础设施提供商之⼀，其 15 年、 29.4 亿美元的租约在规模
上颇具说服⼒，但投机级的信⽤评级使其⽆法提供与亚⻢逊、微软等超⼤规模云⼚商同等的信
⽤背书。
对债券投资者⽽⾔，这份合同的可持续性⾄关重要。⼀旦 CoreWeave 在租约存续期内出现经营
困难或违约，项⽬的现⾦流⽀撑将骤然丧失，⽽专⽤于 AI 计算的数据中⼼资产在再招租⽅⾯的灵
活性有限，资产变现的不确定性将显著上升。

融资成本趋严： AI 数据中⼼的结构性挑战
据彭博报道，此次发⾏是近期 AI 基础设施垃圾债浪潮的组成部分，⽽投资者对此类项⽬要求的收
益率正在系统性抬升，尤其是在租⼾不具备投资级信⽤评级的情况下。
这⼀趋势对后续 AI 数据中⼼项⽬的融资可⾏性构成直接压⼒。以 9.25% 的借贷成本测算，项⽬运
营期内的债务服务负担将显著⾼于具备投资级租⼾的同类项⽬，这将直接压缩开发商的利润空

间，并对项⽬内部回报率形成拖累。
若 AI 算⼒供需格局出现变化，或 CoreWeave 等头部算⼒租⼾的信⽤状况发⽣恶化，市场对此类
债券的定价可能进⼀步⾛⾼，部分融资⽅案的经济性将⾯临重新检验。对于计划通过资本市场
为 AI 基础设施融资的开发商⽽⾔，租⼾的信⽤质量正在成为越来越难以绕开的核⼼变量。

## Document 242: ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD_ September 21 - 25

Subject: ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD: September 21 - 25
Date:    16 hours ago
 The Federal Reserve raised the federal funds rate (FFR) by 25bps on Wednesday, lifting the target range to
3.75%-4.00%. The vote was unanimous. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh cited stronger growth, insufficient progress on

returning fully to target until 2029, while growth and employment forecasts improved. Global bond yields eased a
bit. President Trump hosts Chinese President Xi Jinping in

‐

Yardeni QuickTakes
ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD: September 21 - 25
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 20 Sept 2026 View in browser
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Monica Sauro / Unsplash

### Page 3

The Federal Reserve raised the federal funds rate (FFR) by 25bps on Wednesday, lifting the target range to
3.75%-4.00%. The vote was unanimous. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh cited stronger growth, insufficient progress on
inflation, and rising geopolitical risk as reasons for the move. The Fed's updated projections show inflation not
returning fully to target until 2029, while growth and employment forecasts improved. Global bond yields eased a
bit.
President Trump hosts Chinese President Xi Jinping in Washington this week, with markets watching for progress

on the export-control truce, the countries' new trade and investment boards, and the long-delayed Taiwan arms
package. Attention also turns to Fedspeak, with nine officials set to hit the tape. Goolsbee, Williams, Jefferson,
Barkin, Barr, Hammack, Paulson, Bowman, and Schmid are all on the calendar.
It's a light week for economic data. Unemployment claims (Thu) and regional business surveys from Richmond
(Tue) and Kansas City (Thu) round out the domestic calendar. Flash PMIs (Wed) will offer an early read on

September activity, both domestically and overseas. Overseas, the Swiss National Bank meets Thursday.
Here's more:
(1) FedSpeak. Fed funds futures now imply 3.1 rate increases over the next 12 months and 1.8 over the next six
(chart). The odds of an October hike stand at roughly 58%, against 42% for a hold. The odds of hikes at both the
October and December meetings are at 44%. With October hike odds still close to a coin flip, this week's remarks

by the various talking Fed heads carry real weight for a potential revision of those numbers.
Kansas City Fed President Jeffrey Schmid, speaking Friday, supported this week's hike, saying elevated inflation
reflects more than just oil prices, with a broad range of goods and services also running hot. He described the
labor market as balanced and growth as solid. He is not a voter on the FOMC this year.
The 2-year Treasury yield climbed to 4.75% by Friday's close, up from 4.67% a day earlier, and remains well

above the FFR (chart).

### Page 4

(2) Unemployment Claims. Initial jobless claims (Thu), covering the week ended September 18, follow last
week's 196,000 print, the lowest since July and a break from five straight weeks above the 200,000 mark. That
reading came in below the four-week average of 203,200 (chart). Continuing claims eased to 1,730,000 for the
week ended September 4, with the four-week average at 1,774,000 (chart).
(3) Regional Business Surveys. The Federal Reserve banks of Richmond and Kansas City release their districts’

September business surveys this week. The regional M-PMI business activity index continues to track the
national M-PMI, which eased to 54.6 in August from July's 55.6 (chart).

### Page 5

(4) Flash PMIs. According to S&P Global's survey, August's final manufacturing PMI came in at 53.9, up from a
flash reading of 53.2. Services eased to a final 56.5, down from its flash print of 56.8 (chart). September's flash
PMIs (Wed) are expected to ease slightly from those levels.
Global flash and final manufacturing readings tracked closely in August. Japan's flash reading led at 55.1, while
France's lagged at 48.0 (chart).

### Page 6

(5) Durable Goods Orders. August's new orders for durable goods (Fri) likely rose to yet another record high as
a result of the AI capital spending boom. Most of the major durable goods industries are benefiting from the AI
buildout (chart).
(6) Global Interest Rates. The Swiss National Bank (Thu) is expected to hold its policy rate at 0.00% (chart).

### Page 7

## Document 253: Fed builds credibility with hike; one more likely in Dec

Source PDF: all report pdf/Fed builds credibility with hike; one more likely in Dec.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Market Watch)
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Fed builds credibility with 
hike; one more likely in Dec 
Summary 
Event: The US Federal Reserve hiked its key interest rate by 
25bps to 3.75-4.0%, in a unanimous decision. This marks the 
first hike since 2023, reversing the easing cycle that had 
taken rates down from 4.25% in September 2025 to a trough 
of 3.75% by December 2025. The revised projections 
indicate the Fed expects to raise rates by an additional 25bps 
this year, hold rates in 2027, and cut 25bps in both 2028 and 
2029. It upgraded the long-run rate slightly to 3.2%.  
Market reaction: The US yield curve mildly flattened , while 
the S&P500 index fell 0.5%. The policy rate sensitive US 2 -
year government bond yield jumped 7 bps to 4. 74% as 
markets factored in  three more 25bps hikes by June 2027 . 
The US 10-year yield rose 2bps to 5.02%, its highest since 
2007, while the 30-year yield was little changed. The USD 
index (DXY) rose 0.6%, breaking past its 50- and 100-DMAs. 
Investment strategy: Today’s move realises what we had 
been flagging in recent weeks i.e. the rising risk of a Fed rate 
hike following Fed Chair Warsh’s Jackson Hole speech and 
recent economic data prints, namely: a pick-up in job creation 
and inflation over the summer and a sharp rebound in oil 
prices due to the re-escalation of the Middle East conflict.  
This hike helps the Fed rebuild credibility as an inflation 
fighter. Given the unanimous decision to hike rates this week, 
we believe there will likely be one more hike before the end 
of this year, and one more in H1 2027 as we expect robust 
growth amid strong AI investments and a tight labour market, 
keeping inflation elevated.  That scenario would still be less 
than the three more rate hikes money markets are pricing 
after the latest Fed move. We believe policy rates can return 
to the 4.0 –4.25% range by end-2027, with the possibility of 
cuts in H2 2027  as the impact of tariffs and high oil prices 
start to fade towards the second half of 2027.   
Today’s refreshed Fed policy rate path is mildly bullish for the 
USD index; so we revise up our 3 -month target to 100. We 
also revise up our 3-month 10-year yield target to 5.0-5.25%.  
 The USD rose, while stocks fell after the Fed hiked rates 
and signalled one more rate hike this year; ultra-long 
bonds rose slightly as the Fed rebuild its inflation-
fighting credentials

2026-27 and inflation projection for 2026; however, it 
still sees inflation cooling over the next two years

### Page 2

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 17 September 2026

Backdrop to the rate hike 
In hiking rates, Fed Chair Warsh reaffirmed the inflation 
threat to the US economy. US headline inflation accelerated 
to 0.4% m/m in August and stayed elevated on an annualised 
basis at 3.4% y/y. Energy prices , driven by the Middle East 
conflict, were a primary contributor. Oil prices have extended 
gains since the August inflation report, surging past USD 
100/bbl. Meanwhile, core inflation rose 0.3% m/m in August, 
beating estimates for a 0.2% rise . Also, US producer prices 
jumped 0.4% m/m in August, the most since May, and 5.4%  
y/y, further reinforcing the inflationary backdrop. 
The inflation report follow ed a stronger -than-expected jobs 
report for August. US monthly job creation has surged to an 
average 80,000 this year, from 10,000, tightening a job 
market that already faces a supply crunch from the Trump 
administration’s clampdown against immigration.  The 
combination of an acceleration in job creation and inflation 
over the summer, combined with a rebound in oil prices, 
supported our expectation for a Fed rate hike this week.  
Removing “a dose of accommodation” 
Warsh framed the decision to hike as removing "a dose of 
accommodation", rather than the start of an aggressive rate 
hiking cycle, and we agree with that characterisation. The aim 
is to bring financial and credit conditions more in line with the 
Fed's price stability objectives. To quote Warsh: “Our 
predominant focus is on the price stability side of our 
mandate. The plain fact is that inflation is too high, and has 
been for too long….This  summer’s inflation readings do not 
tell me that underlying trends have meaningfully improved.” 
He added that many categories in the last consumer and 
producer inflation reports are still showing price increases 
above 3% on a six- and 12-month basis.  
The unanimous vote to hike rates, contrasts with a 9 -3 split 
vote in July to hold rates. When combined with the pencilling 
in of one more hike by December, this suggests a majority of 
Fed policymakers have turned more hawkish over the 
summer. In doing so, they have helped rebuild the Fed’s 
inflation-fighting credentials and its independence against 
President Trump’s pressures to cut rates. This explains why 
long-end bond yields were stable after this week’s hike. 
Nevertheless, Warsh provided little guidance on the future 
rate path, as he held what was reportedly the shortest post -
meeting press conference on record for a Fed chair . This is 
consistent with his stated preference for less central bank 
communication. We expect more clarity once the Fed task 
forces submit their reports by year end. 
— Jonathan Liang, CIO for Fixed Income and FX   
Rajat Bhattacharya, Senior Investment Strategist

## Document 258: First impressions_ NZ GDP, June quarter 2026 (2026-09-26)

Source PDF: all report pdf/First impressions_ NZ GDP, June quarter 2026 (2026-09-26).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-09-26
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#gdp #newzealand
First impressions: NZ GDP, June quarter 2026
By Michael Gordon
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
09:01 September 17 2026 Share
The New Zealand economy grew by 0.2% in the June quarter, in line with our forecast,
although it was a little softer in the details than we expected.
Key results
Quarterly change: +0.2% (last: +0.9%, revised from +0.8%)
Westpac f/c: +0.2%, market f/c: +0.1%, RBNZ: 0.0%

### Page 2

Annual change: +2.6% (last: +1.7%, revised from +1.5%)
Westpac f/c: +2.4%
The June quarter national accounts reinforce our message that the New Zealand economy has largely
held its ground through the US-Iran conflict, though it hasn’t been completely unscathed. A few sectors
in particular have taken the brunt of the surge in fuel prices since March, but the greater impact is
probably more a matter of what might have been: the momentum that was building in late 2025 and

early 2026 might have progressed further were it not for this latest headwind.
GDP rose by 0.2% for the quarter, in line with our forecast. This was slightly ahead of the median
market forecast of 0.1% and the RBNZ’s forecast of a flat outturn in their September Monetary Policy
Statement (which didn’t have the benefit of the final data releases that led us and the market to
upgrade our forecasts).
As expected, the largest growth contribution came from construction, up 2.7%. There were also solid

contributions from manufacturing, wholesaling, and information and telecommunications. The main
declines were in transport and hospitality, which likely reflects the impact of the surge in fuel prices –
households have tightened their belts by cutting back on travel in particular.
It was a ‘high’ read for the quarter – 0.247% unrounded – and March quarter growth was revised up
from 0.8% to 0.9%, largely due to updated data on construction activity. Together these meant that

annual growth accelerated to 2.6%, ahead of our 2.4% estimate.
However, the results for the June quarter were a little weaker in the details than we were expecting.
Firstly, the non-additive balancing item (as we calculate it, which is a little different from what Stats NZ
reports) knocked around 0.2ppts off growth for the quarter, less than the 0.3ppts that we had assumed.
Furthermore, we were surprised to the downside on balance for the ‘market’ sectors of the economy,

with softer than expected results in agriculture, transport and administrative services. The latter two will
likely reflect the impact of higher fuel prices on travel and tourism (admin services includes tour
operators and travel agencies). In contrast, there were surprisingly large gains in healthcare (up 0.8%)
and central government administration (up 2.1%, though this came after a 0.8% fall in the March
quarter).
For the RBNZ, these results will help to assuage some of the committee members’ concerns about

downside risks to growth. But our view remains that the committee will have more of an eye to the
evidence on inflation pressures, such as the NZIER business survey on the 6  of October and the CPI
on the 22 , as well as the rapidly shifting picture for oil prices, to determine their next move.
Michael Gordon, Senior Economist

## Document 270: Global Expansion of Chinese Construction Machinery_ Lessons from Brazil (2026-09-14)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Expansion of Chinese Construction Machinery_ Lessons from Brazil (2026-09-14).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Global Expansion of Chinese 
Construction Machinery:  
Lessons from Brazil

1 
Authors 
Deyun Ou 
Economist

Yi Xiong, Ph.D. 
Chief Economist

September 14, 2026 
Executive Summary 
China’s exports have been strong this year, surging by 19% YoY in the first 
eight months. Among these exports, heavy-asset capital goods have played a 
particularly important role. Using construction machinery as a representative 
example, we illustrate how stronger product competitiveness, Chinese overseas 
projects, and Chinese manufacturers’ “going-global” strategies have enabled 
market share gains across a broad range of economies. 
Chinese construction machinery has expanded across the globe since 2020. 
Exports of related products quadrupled between 2020 and 2025 and have 
remained on a strong upward trend this year. More importantly, Chinese 
products have achieved significant market-share gains across a broad range of 
economies and accounted for 26% of global imports in 2025 compared to 10% in 
2019. In this report, we aim to explore the factors behind this. 
We use Chinese excavator exports to Brazil as a case study. Excavators are 
one of the most representative categories of construction machinery, while Brazil 
is South America’s largest excavator importer and ranks among the world’s top 
20. Chinese excavators have also made remarkable market-share gains in Brazil, 
with their share rising from 20% to 90% in six years. Brazil therefore offers a 
compelling case study on this topic. 
Through the case study and our analysis of aggregate data, we identify three 
drivers of the global expansion of Chinese construction machinery: 
— Product competitiveness: Chinese brands have steadily improved both 
price advantage and product quality. 
— Demand from Chinese overseas projects: Chinese contractors are 
undertaking more infrastructure projects and naturally exhibit stronger 
preferences for Chinese equipment. 
— “Going global” strategy by Chinese companies: Higher profitability has 
encouraged them to direct more resources towards overseas expansion.

### Page 2

14 September 2026 
China Macro

Deutsche Bank AG/Hong Kong Page 2

Introduction 
China’s exports have been strong this year, surging by 19% YoY in the first eight 
months. In a note published for clients in March, we identified several drivers of 
this export growth, including AI-related demand, energy-cost advantages and the 
green transition. In particular, heavy-asset capital goods have been an important 
contributor, with construction machinery offering a representative example. 
Chinese construction machinery has expanded rapidly across the globe since 
2020. Using HS8429 products —including excavators, bulldozers, graders and 
related equipment —as a proxy, export values quadrupled between 2020 and 
2025 (Figure 1). More importantly, the expansion has been broad -based: 128 of 
the 140 economies reporting HS8429 imports recorded an increase in China’s 
market share over the period (Figure 2). Chinese products now account for more 
than half of HS8429 imports in over 30 economies. As a result, China’s share of 
global HS8429 imports has risen from 10% in 2019 to 26% in 2025. 
Looking at  aggregate trade data alone  does not fully reveal the multiple 
underlying drivers. At the country level, economic structures, investment cycles 
and infrastructure demand vary substantially, leading to very different import 
patterns and market-share dynamics. At the product level, HS8429 encompasses 
a diverse range of machinery categories with different technologies, end-markets 
and competitive landscapes.  
Therefore, we adopt a case -study approach and focus on Chinese excavator 
exports to Brazil. We believe this is particularly representative since:  
• Excavators are highly representative of the construction machinery 
industry, accounting for half of China’s HS8429 exports. 
• Brazil is the largest excavator importer in South America and ranks 
among the top twenty importers globally. The demand is underpinned by 
three factors. 1) As a major mining economy, Brazil requires substantial 
earthmoving equipment. 2) Brazil is one of the world's largest agricultural 
producers. The farming sector relies heavily on capital investment and 
machinery to raise productivity. 3) The government has renewed its focus 
on infrastructure development in recent years.  
• Chinese excavator manufacturers have achieved exceptional market -
share gains  in Brazil. Their  share increased from 20% to 90% over the 
past six years  (Figure 4), with gains observed across all excavator 
segments.

14 September 2026 
China Macro

Deutsche Bank AG/Hong Kong Page 3

Expansion of Chinese Excavators in Brazil  
Chinese excavators have become increasingly visible across Brazil . Their 
presence is no longer confined to mines, railways, and other construction sites. 
They are increasingly involved in landmark projects such as Rio Olympic venues 
and World Cup stadiums, and can even be found in residential backyards and 
farms. Chinese brands reportedly occupied the top three positions in Brazil’s 
market in 2025, while six Chinese brands ranked among the top ten. Customs 
data paint a stronger picture: Chinese products now account for ~90% of Brazil’s 
excavator imports, both by value and volume. 
China’s expansion appears to have been supported mainly by Brazil’s incremental 
demand rather than the outright displacement of competing suppliers. During the 
market recovery from 2017 to 2021, excavator imports rose from all major 
sources. From 2022 to 2025, imports from China grew much faster, driving a 
rapid increase in China’s market share, while annual imports from other 
suppliers—including Euro pe, Japan and Korea —remained broadly stable at 
around USD 100mn in total  (Figure 5). This does not mean that other suppliers

### Page 4

14 September 2026 
China Macro

Deutsche Bank AG/Hong Kong Page 4

were unaffected by Chinese competition: without China’s sharp market -share 
gains, they would likely have captured a larger share of Brazil’s surge in excavator 
demand. 
Interestingly, the expansion has been different across segments  (Figure 6). 
Medium-sized excavators began gaining market share steadily after 2017, while 
mini excavators only entered a similar trajectory after 2019. Large excavators 
followed a different path. Prior to 2021, Chinese brands were absent, yet they 
rapidly gained traction after the first entry. The distinct patterns suggest that the 
rise of Chinese excavators might have been driven by multiple demand - and 
supply-side forces.

Figure 5: This expansion appears to have come largely 
from incremental demand rather than outright 
displacement

Figure 6: Expansion has been different across product 
segments

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Brazil Custom

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Brazil Custom. Brazil’s 8-digit NCM customs 
classification distinguishes excavators by power categories, allowing us to group them 
into three segments.

Factor 1: Price Advantage Meets Quality Upgrading  
A widening price advantage has been a major driver behind the expansion of 
Chinese products in the medium-sized and mini excavator segments. Since these 
products are relatively less technologically demanding, pricing remains a major 
consideration for buyers.  We divide the sample into four periods and  compare 
China’s price advantage against market share gains (Figure 7). China’s advantage 
has steadily expanded, with products now more than 50% cheaper than peers. 
Meanwhile, the relationship between price advantage and market share is highly 
significant. For medium -sized excavators, a one percentage point increase in 
China’s price advantage is associated with a 0.4pt gain in market share. For mini 
excavators, the corresponding relationship is even higher at 0.9pt. 
Indeed, mini excavators provide the clearest example of price -driven expansion. 
These machines are used for small -scale construction projects or purchased by 
individual consumers, making demand particularly price -sensitive. Chinese 
products began to undercut competitors after 2017, and their price advantage 
widened significantly after 2020  (Figure 8). The gap has now reached USD 
17,000 per unit . We further decompose this price advantage  (Figure 9). 60% of 
the widening gap reflects price increases by overseas competitors amid higher 
inflation, while 40% reflects price cuts by Chinese manufacturers. RMB

### Page 5

14 September 2026 
China Macro

Deutsche Bank AG/Hong Kong Page 5

depreciation accounted for 10% of the gap during 2023-25. However, following 
its appreciation in 2026, the cumulative contribution has turned negative. 
More noteworthy is the advance of Chinese brands into the high -end segment. 
Large excavators are far more technologically sophisticated. Before 2021, 
Chinese brands were virtually absent from Brazil’s large excavators market. They 
entered the market in 2021 and expanded rapidly thereafter (Figure 10). So far in 
2026, 43 of Brazil’s 47 imported large excavators have come from China. Chinese 
manufacturers are reportedly delivering 100-tonne-class excavators to Brazilian 
mining customers and are actively promoting equipment in the 500-tonne 
category. Chinese companies ’ competitiveness is increasingly extending from 
price advantage toward technology intensity.

Figure 7: A widening price advantage has driven 
expansion in the medium-sized and mini excavator

of price-driven expansion

the periods 2006-2010, 2011-2015, 2016-2020, and 2021-2025, respectively.

Factor 2: Demand from Chinese Overseas Projects  
Chinese firms are undertaking more construction projects in Brazil, generating 
additional demand. They naturally have a stronger preference for Chinese-made 
machinery. The value of construction projects completed by Chinese companies 
has risen consistently since 2021. Growth in newly signed contracts has been

### Page 6

14 September 2026 
China Macro

Deutsche Bank AG/Hong Kong Page 6

even stronger, with their value reaching a record USD 4.6bn in 2024  (Figure 11). 
This trend may have contributed to the growth in excavator imports since 2024, 
especially for large excavators. 
The Salvador -Itaparica Bridge project provides a compelling example. Once 
completed, it will be the longest sea -crossing bridge in South America. The 
project was awarded to two Chinese SOEs in 2019, with an initial investment of 
USD 2.2bn, which was raised to USD 2.7bn in 2025. It is the largest infrastructure 
investment undertaken by Chinese companies in Brazil. Since construction began 
this year, ~800 tons of equipment from China have arrived at the port of Salvador 
since April, consistent with the surge in custom data (Figure 12). Most recently on 
July 1, the project company showcased the first batch of equipment deployed on 
site, all sourced from China.

Figure 11: Chinese firms are undertaking more 
construction projects in Brazil

Figure 12: Imports of Chinese machinery through the 
Port of Salvador have surged in recent months

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, China Mofcom

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Brazil Custom

Factor 3: Supply-Side Expansion Driven by Higher Profitability 
Chinese firms have strong incentives to expand overseas because foreign 
markets offer higher profitability. Some views have argued that low export prices 
primarily reflect dumping driven by weak domestic demand. If that were the case, 
corporate profits would be expected to remain flat or deteriorate as firms 
sacrificed margins to gain market share. Company -level evidence suggests the 
opposite. 
We examine five listed Chinese construction machinery manufacturers.  
Combined overseas revenue has risen consistently since 2020, doubling in 2022 
and doubling again by 2025, broadly mirroring the trajectory of China’s total 
construction machinery exports (Figure 13). More importantly, overseas 
profitability has improved substantially. Across the five companies, operating 
margins in overseas markets have increased by 5-15 percentage points, with the 
average rising from 20% during the previous decade to 30% today (Figure 14). In 
contrast, domestic margins have fallen by 5 percentage points.

### Page 7

14 September 2026 
China Macro

Deutsche Bank AG/Hong Kong Page 7

Figure 13: Overseas revenue has risen consistently 
since 2021

Figure 14: Overseas profit margins have exceeded 
domestic margins

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Wind

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Wind

Beyond Excavators: Chinese Construction Machinery 
Expansion 
Through this case study, we identify three forces underpinning the expansion of 
Chinese excavators in Brazil: improving price and quality competitiveness, 
growing demand from Chinese overseas projects, and proactive going -global 
strategies by Chinese companies. 
We believe the forces extend to the broader construction machinery sector.   
• Price and Quality Competitiveness. The price advantage over HS8429 
has become more pronounced since 2022  (Figure 15). The analysis of 
eight-digit categories revealed a more comprehensive picture  (Figure 
16). First, exports increased across all categories, including those with 
rising prices. Second, the products recording larger price increases 
tended to have higher unit values. That said, the expansion is driven by 
price cuts as well as increasing demand for higher-value products.  
• Overseas Expansion of Chinese Enterprises . Chinese overseas 
construction project s have reaccelerated since 2024, with completed 
value and newly signed contracts rising by 20% and 15%, respectively  
(Figure 17). Belt and Road economies are key destinations for Chinese 
overseas construction projects, and Chinese construction machinery has 
gained market share much more rapidly in these markets. 
• Proactive “Going global” strategy . Since the 2010s, construction 
machinery manufacturers have viewed overseas expansion as a key pillar 
of future growth and have invested heavily in international distribution 
channels, service networks and localized production facilities. 
Looking ahead, we believe all three drivers are likely to remain in place for an 
extended period. China’s manufacturing capability and continued technological 
progress should support further gains in product competitiveness. Accelerating 
component localization could further strengthen the price advantage. Meanwhile, 
the overseas expansion of Chinese companies is expected to remain a long-term 
trend. Belt and Road economies warrant particular attention, as Chinese

### Page 8

14 September 2026 
China Macro

Deutsche Bank AG/Hong Kong Page 8

investment in these markets has surged in recent years, laying a solid foundation 
for further overseas expansion (Figure 18). As a result, we remain constructive on 
the outlook for construction machinery exports.  
That said, several risks warrant monitoring. First, further RMB appreciation could 
erode the price competitiveness of Chinese products. Second, the sharp increase 
in market share achieved in Brazil —from 20% to 90% —may not be easily 
replicated elsewhere. In some markets, Chinese firms could face stron ger 
competition from local manufacturers, limiting the pace of further market -share 
gains.

become more pronounced since 2022

increasing demand for higher-value products

SPECIAL EDITION | HOW BAS IS THE RISE 
IN BOND YIELDS?

TRANSCRIPTION

0:00:10 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Everyone, welcome. As we head into autumn 2026, interest rates are reaching levels never seen for many, many years. 
Why is this happening? Is it sustainable? And what are the implications for the economies of different countries? All these 
questions will be discussed over the next few minutes with the economists of BNP Paribas. Let us begin with Isabelle 
Mateos y Lago, chief economist of the group. Hello, Isabelle.  
0:00:38 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everyone. 
0:00:39 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you for being with us today. Just to begin, a simple explanation. What rates are we talking about and which countries 
are affected? 
0:00:48 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, it is very important to clarify this. So, we are talking about long-term interest rates, rates at 10 years or longer 
maturities, 30 years, etc. The rates at which sovereign states borrow on the market. And this is primarily something that is 
happening in advanced countries, so United States, United King dom, Japan, and then the Eurozone countries. And indeed, 
as you said, we are seeing since the beginning of the summer, interest rates reach levels we had not seen for about 20 
years. But it is important to keep in mind that up until that point, up until the mid -2000s, the level of interest rates that 
we're seeing now were really quite common, quite normal, so one shouldn't overdramatize.  
0:01:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why do we find ourselves in this situation and how did we get to this point?  
0:01:41 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, so the direct cause is that investors are demanding higher interest rates in order to  lend their capital to governments. 
But why is this? Well, there's essentially three reasons for this, and not all of them are bad, and they're not mutually 
exclusive either. The top reason to my mind is that there's just greater competition for capital be tween sovereign states 
and the corporate sector, who are all facing simultaneously additional investment needs, in defense, in artificial 
intelligence, in energy transition, in resilience. And so,  you just have greater competition for capital and that pushes up the 
price of capital, which is the interest rate. 
Reason number two is because markets have revised upwards their expectations of how high central banks are going to be 
taking their interest rates. 
And this mechanically translates into higher rates at the long end. And then there's a third reason, which doesn't apply 
necessarily in every case, but which is essentially higher sovereign risk, a kind of a risk premium, in cases where there are  
concerns arising about the sustainability of these public debts.

0:02:59 - Emmanuel Laborde 
What are the implications for global economies? 
0:03:03 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
So that's exactly what we'll be discussing in the next segments. But basically, the two key issues are, one, an adverse 
impact on public finances. It's higher interest rates making it harder to balance the books in terms of public accounts. And 
then secondly, these higher rates tend to slow down economic growth. Of course, by how much and how serious it will be, 
depends on how high interest rates go and how long they stay there. But that's for the next segments.  
0:03:38 - Emmanuel Laborde

Hello Hélène and welcome into this special edition. 
0:03:58 - Hélène Baudchon 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everybody. 
0:04:01 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When economists talk about public debt, they often refer to the sustainability of debt. As an economist, can you explain to 
us what that means and what is the debt trajectory deemed unsustainable?  
0:04:15 - Hélène Baudchon 
To begin with, I will say that a temporary increase in debt can be seen as a normal evolution, not particularly concerning, 
for instance during an economic crisis or more recently during a health crisis. In such cases, it acts as a buffer. A problem  
arises when the debt level never comes back down. An unsustainable trajectory can be therefore defined, characterized by 
a continuous regular rise in the debt level, both in absolute terms and as a proportion of GDP, driven by high and recurring 
fiscal deficits. And sometimes interest payments can exacerbate the problem.  
0:05:00 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In very simple terms, you are talking about the snowball effect.  
0:05:03 - Hélène Baudchon 
Exactly explaining why consolidation measures are needed in order to prevent such an adverse situation from occurring 
and in order to prevent, in fact, a sudden loss of confidence from creditors.  
0:05:16 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Regarding governments, what should serve as a warning sign for them? And at what point could the situation become, let's 
say, problematic? 
0:05:27 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, simply speaking, when interest rates rise, not so long ago, up until 2022, Eurozone countries were borrowing at very 
low rates and in some instance, even negative rates. And although debt levels were already at high level at that time, the 
low rates helped manage debt servicing. Not much fiscal effort was needed to keep the public debt ratios in check.  
0:05:55 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Of course, we all remember this because it was a very unusual situation, but now things are very different.

### Page 3

3

0:06:01 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed, the situation today is clearly more challenging simply because bond yields are significantly higher and they 
are rising at the time of our discussion. This is for the part the result of the ECB's monetary tightening in 2022 -2023 aimed 
at addressing the inflation shock then. Then long-term interest rates have risen as a consequence and have aligned more 
closely with GDP growth rate. And in technical terms, this is what we refer to as a narrowing of the "r" - "g" spread. 
0:06:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why is this spread so important? Why do you, the economists, monitor it this way?  
0:06:48 - Hélène Baudchon 
Essentially, you have the interest rate "r", which determines the debt burden, while the growth rate "g" determines the 
trajectory of fiscal revenues. And if "r" is lower than "g", and this has been the case for many years, as you can see on the  
chart on the screen, it means that interest payments are increasing at a slower pace than the revenue component of the 
equation. 
0:07:14 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this provides some leeway in other budgetary areas, such as education, of course, defense, maybe pensions.  
0:07:22 - Hélène Baudchon

economy isn't growing that much faster, then fiscal leeway diminishes.

rates across all Eurozone countries. Is France the only country seeing its public debt go up?  
No, it's not, Emmanuel. This is happening in the United Kingdom and in the United States. These economies, like France, 
are running public deficits that are ever close or exceed 5% of GDP, which is too large for debt ratio to stabilize on its ow n. 
France and the United Kingdom are trying to gradually reduce their public deficit so as not to compromise growth. The 
United States is not going on that path yet, but market pressure could eventually force them to do so.

significantly and that renders the sovereign yield a less relevant benchmark.  
And now the most relevant benchmark is the swap rate because it determines the cost of bank funding that backs new 
loans. 
0:13:56 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Since the end of 2024, we have seen a rise in long-term 10-year swap rates. What impact does this have on bank lending 
rates? 
0:14:08 - Thomas Humblot 
Fixed loans have obviously become more expensive, but to a lesser extent than if they were priced solely based on long -
term sovereign rates. And what about existing fixed-rate loans? 
There is no impact. An increase in rates for the entire maturity of a loan does not have any effect if it is a fixed -rate loan. 
Borrowers with outstanding loans are therefore protected. Obviously, new fixed loans are granted at higher rates. And this 
sharply contrasts with a floating-rates loans. 
Because with floating-rates loans, borrowers experience regular rate adjustments throughout the term of the loan. And in 
terms of bank financing? Strictly in terms of bank financing, an increase in short -term rates has an immediate and 
widespread restrictive impact on borrowers, while for long-term interest rates, the impact is more gradual, and it comes 
gradually as the outstanding amount of fixed-rate loans are progressively renewed. And the thing is that since the 
beginning of the war in Iran, we have seen a combination of the two.  
And in practical terms, the rate of new bank loans to non-financial corporations has increased about 25 basis points in the 
eurozone since the beginning of the year. 
0:16:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Just a few words about companies that borrow directly from the market.  
0:16:16 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, in that case, the impact is even more straightforward. The rate at which a company borrows from the market is the 
risk-free rate plus a premium. 
0:16:28 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Have risk premiums, known as corporate credit spreads, also increased?  
0:16:34 - Thomas Humblot 
Yes, they have increased temporarily at the beginning of the year but have returned to normal since. For the time being, the 
widening remains moderate for the highest-rated investment-grade issuers. 
However, some significant bond issuance in the euro debt market due to some major US technology companies has 
increased spreads for longer maturities. 
0:17:11 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In particular terms, how does this affect the investment decisions of the companies?  
0:17:19 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, investments that were previously worthwhile due to returns sufficient to cover the cost of capital may no longer do so 
and in turn that might lead to a decrease in demand for financing.  
0:17:40 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much Thomas. Now let us move on to the emerging economies. And it is with Cynthia Kalasopatan -Antoine.

Hello Cynthia, and welcome. 
0:17:54 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Hi, Emmanuel. Hello, everyone. 
0:17:57 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Long-term bond yields rose significantly in advanced economies over the summer. What can we now say about emerging 
markets? 
0:18:06 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Emerging bond markets held up well, which is quite unusual. The rise in long -term sovereign yields in emerging countries 
has been less pronounced than in advanced economies, including the United States and Germany. Now, there are two 
main exceptions, Turkey and Poland. Meanwhile, we did not witness any significant correction in emerging financial 
markets over the summer, and the main currencies appreciated slightly against the dollar or the euro.  
0:18:37 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Does this mean that public finances are more robust in emerging countries?  
0:18:43 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, it depends. You see, in cases like the Czech Republic and South Korea, public debt is moderate and macroeconomic 
fundamentals are solid. However, these countries are now less frequently considered as emerging and tend to be viewed 
as newly advanced economies. By contrast, Brazil and South Africa have weaker public finances and investors have 
already incorporated a higher sovereign risk into their assessments. And this is reflected in the bond yields. And this 
assessment of sovereign risk did not really change over the summer. 
0:19:26 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When comparing the main fiscal metrics, what are the trends? What do you see as an economist?  
0:19:33 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
On average, the fiscal metrics in emerging markets are not substantially worse than those in advanced economies. The 
average budget deficit levels are similar, ranging from 4% to 5% of GDP in 2025 -2026, according to the IMF. And as in 
advanced economies, the public debt is higher than its pre-Covid level. However, the average public debt to GDP ratio in 
emerging countries excluding China is close to 60%, whereas in advanced economies, it is above 100%.  
0:20:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Do you arrive at the same conclusion when comparing debt sustainability criteria?  
0:20:17 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, there are significant regional differences. In emerging Asia, the factors that determine debt sustainability are more 
often favorable than those in advanced economies. In Central Europe, the gap between the interest rate paid on debt and 
the GDP growth rate is also favorable. However, the primary deficits in some countries are high.  
0:20:45 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In a few words, a brief overview of the situation in Latin America.  
0:20:49 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, Brazil and Mexico are in a less favorable position. These countries would indeed need to generate a significant 
primary budget surplus each year in order to stabilize their debt in the medium term.

0:21:07 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Economists use the term original sin, which refers to the situation when governments resort to borrowing in foreign 
currency to address the shortfall in domestic financing. Does this phenomenon continue to distinguish emerging 
countries? 
0:21:24 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Yes, this is still the case. Emerging countries still have a significant share of their debt in foreign currency. This repres ents 
about 35 to 40% of their total debt on average. And in fact, now, this figure has decreased over the past decade, and this 
contributes to their resilience. However, governments in emerging markets have a less favorable position as regards their 
debt profile and their financing costs are also on average higher.  
0:22:02 - Emmanuel Laborde

0:22:07 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
China's public debt is significant and growing rapidly. That being said, it is denominated in domestic currency and issued in  
domestic markets. This debt is, however, sustainable in the medium term due to a very low effective interest rate and a 
high growth rate.

0:22:58 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Absolutely not. The main contribution that central banks can bring to dealing with this situation is to restore price stabili ty, 
as is their mandate, as promptly as possible. And in the current circumstances, this means keeping relatively high policy 
rates, in some cases, rising them further. And the responsibility for keeping public finances on a sustainable footing rests 
squarely with governments and any attempt by these governments to instead take the easy way out by pressuring central 
banks to lower interest rates prematurely would be enormously counterproductive.

Rapid improvements in AI capabilities and growing corporate 
efforts to integrate the technology into enterprise workflows 
have raised concerns that AI could trigger a “job apocalypse”, 
especially as some companies have cited AI as a factor in 
recent layoffs (see pg. 16), entry-level hiring appears to be 
softening in some AI-exposed occupations, and unemployment 
among college graduates has risen above the pre-pandemic 
average. So, just how concerned should we be about the 
disruptive potential of AI for the US labor market? 
Some prominent technologists predict that AI could ultimately 
eliminate a massive number of jobs. They argue that, unlike 
previous technological shifts that primarily automated routine 
manual labor and clerical tasks, increasingly capable AI models 
will soon be able to handle much of the cognitive work humans 
perform, leaving many workers with less to do. They also point 
to the unprecedented speed of AI advances, which could 
outpace the labor market’s natural ability to adapt, leading to 
substantial displacement in as soon as the next few years. And 
even if the technology eventually creates new roles, they warn 
of a skills mismatch, which could mean that these new roles 
will be filled by AI itself rather than displaced human workers. 
GS Senior Global Economist Joseph Briggs also expects 
significant labor displacement, estimating more than 9% of the 
labor force—around 15mn workers—could lose their jobs during 
the 10-year AI transition he assumes. But he believes these 
losses will prove temporary owing to his expectation that AI will 
create many new jobs over the long term even as it destroys 
existing ones, taking comfort from the historical precedent of 
technological innovations creating much new work and the 
dynamism of the US economy (which GS CEO David Solomon 
also cites as a key reason why he believes the narrative of an AI 
job apocalypse is overblown). And the 10-year AI transition 
period that Briggs assumes should provide the labor market 
time to adapt.  
Neil Thompson, Director of the FutureTech research project at 
MIT’s Computer Science and AI lab, is less convinced that AI 
will drive large-scale job displacement. He explains that 
“capability is only one step in the process that leads to labor 
market change”—AI must also perform reliably, have access to 
the right information, and be cost effective to meaningfully 
impact jobs. And because most jobs consist of many tasks, only 
some of which can be automated, he expects a slower and 
more uneven labor market adjustment than AI’s current 
capabilities suggest. Thompson also takes some—though not 
complete—comfort from previous automation waves that 
created many new jobs. So, he describes AI’s impact on the 
labor market more as a “rising tide” than a “crashing wave”: 
real change and churn, especially if adoption accelerates 
quickly, but not sudden, widespread job loss.   
We then spoke again with Daron Acemoglu, Institute Professor 
at MIT and winner of the Nobel Prize in Economics. He expects 
AI to have only a small net negative impact on labor over the 
next five years—far less than some technologists predict—as 
current AI models are more likely to replace than complement 
workers (GS US Economist Elsie Peng indeed finds that AI is 
currently substituting more than augmenting, resulting in a 
small net drag on the labor market). But Acemoglu warns that 
job losses could be larger over the longer term if AI investment 
continues to focus more on replacing than complementing 
workers, and especially so if agentic advances, improvements 
in AI models’ social capabilities, or AI-robotics integration 
expand the set of jobs that the technology could impact (GS 
equity analysts James Schneider and Luya You also argue that 
falling token costs could make more enterprise workflows 
economically viable to automate). And Acemoglu is less 
comforted than Briggs and Thompson by history, pointing out 
that “no general law of economics says that job creation must 
match job destruction.” 
So, which workers will be most impacted by these labor market 
shifts? While much of the concern about AI-driven job losses 
has centered on new graduates and white-collar workers more 
broadly, GS US Economists Jessica Rindels and Pierfrancesco 
Mei find that AI has had little impact on college graduates’ job 
prospects so far. But they argue that could change over the 
near term as college graduates are disproportionately employed 
in industries with high AI adoption rates and occupations with 
more potentially automatable tasks. Even so, Rindels and Mei 
remain relatively optimistic about college graduates’ longer-term 
prospects, finding that they have historically adjusted more 
nimbly than other workers to technological disruption.  
As for white-collar workers more broadly, Acemoglu argues that 
those performing cognitive, routine tasks under predictable 
conditions—such as customer service and back-office work—
will bear the brunt of AI displacement. As these tasks are 
disproportionately performed by lower-paid white-collar 
workers, he expects this to lead to a rise in income inequality 
over the coming years. However, Acemoglu notes that if 
workers in higher-paid managerial roles are also displaced, as 
some think they could be, then inequality could actually decline.   
Thompson agrees that much will depend on what types of work 
AI ultimately automates, with the key being whether it 
automates the most or least expert parts of a job. If AI 
automates the least expert tasks, employment would fall while 
wages rise as the work becomes more specialized; if it instead 
automates the most expert tasks, employment would rise while 
wages fall as more workers can do the job. That framework 
suggests the impact will cut across the occupation and wage 
distribution rather than hitting one worker group in particular.   
Amid this debate, GS Senior US Equity Strategist Ryan 
Hammond finds that the technology’s impact on corporate labor 
needs—and, crucially, earnings—remains too uncertain for 
markets to reward today. As a result, he says, investors 
continue to favor the AI infrastructure complex, where earnings 
benefits are more tangible. But Hammond expects this to 
change, with the AI trade eventually broadening to include labor 
productivity beneficiaries as clearer evidence that AI’s much-
touted productivity benefits can generate a durable earnings 
uplift emerges.  
Allison Nathan, Editor  
Email: allison.nathan@gs.com     
Tel: 212-357-7504
